### Turn 1 · Claude
I like it, and R360 is one of the few companies that could actually pull it off. The part I'd change is "plug in and done."

**Why it's strong**
- You already run this for big banks. The hard parts (points, rules, fraud, paying out rewards) are 17 years solved.
- Lots of companies (retailers, apps, airlines, telcos) want a program but can't build one.
- AI agents can now do setup work that used to take a team months.

**Where it breaks**
- Plug-in loyalty software already exists (Capillary, Talon.One, Antavo and others). If you're only software, you're one of many.
- Every company's customer data is messy. That's usually where "boom, it's live" falls apart.
- A program isn't really live until rewards get delivered: vouchers, flights, cashback. That needs suppliers, money moving, and customer support, not just tech.

**The real edge**
Sell it as "your program, live in days, with R360's rewards already inside." The software gets you in the door. The rewards network is what they can't copy. It also fits straight into your non-bank push.

One question: who buys first?
1. Mid-size brands (retail, D2C, apps)
2. Big non-bank companies (airlines, telcos)
3. Agencies who'd resell it to their clients

### Turn 8 · Amit
*Sent: Sun 27 Sep 2026, 15:49 IST*
Okay, so what do you think? Is this a solid business model we have?

### Turn 9 · Claude
Yes, it's solid, but only as a package: our agents plus our rewards. If we sell it as software alone, it isn't.

**Why it works**
- **We earn three ways:** a setup fee, a monthly fee per brand, and a cut of every reward redeemed from our catalogue. The reward cut is the big one, and it grows as members use the program.
- **Timing is right.** The tab shows 20+ brands that launched or relaunched a program this year with thin rewards. That's a real, current gap.
- **Hard to copy.** Rivals can build agents in a year. Building rewards partners, delivery and payouts across countries took us 17 years.

**What could break it**
1. **Brands spend less than banks.** A bank pays for points out of card fees. A grocery chain or airline pays out of thin margins, so each customer will bring in less. Fix: price by results (a share of the extra sales we bring in), not a flat fee.
2. **Tech in a week, a yes in six months.** Big brands decide slowly even when setup is fast. Fix: start with mid-size brands that decide in weeks, and use agencies to reach many brands at once.
3. **"Live in days" is not proven yet.** Messy customer data could stretch it to months. Fix: prove it on 3 pilots before we promise it in sales pitches.

**What would make me sure:** 3 paying brands from the list live within 90 days, each set up in under 2 weeks, with members actually redeeming rewards. If that happens, it's a real business. If setup keeps slipping, it's just another loyalty software company.

Want me to add a "How we make money" section with this to the one pager?

### Turn 23 · Claude
Here's how I'd honestly place us, using what the research turned up.

**The short version:** today we're a heavyweight rewards supplier walking into a software fight. Once the agents and playbooks are built (month 4 to 6), we'd be the only one in the room offering all three pieces: software, agents and rewards. Until then, lead with rewards, because that's where we already beat everyone.

**How we compare, pitch by pitch** (my judgment from the research):

| Who | Speed to live | Rewards and delivery | Agents that run it | Shopkeeper tools (WhatsApp, cash, tax) | Non-bank references |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **R360 today** | Rewards shop: 2 weeks (target) | Strong: India, Gulf, Singapore, bank-grade scale | Not built yet | Not built yet | Thin. This is our weak spot |
| **R360 after phase 2** | Full program: ~7 days (target) | Strong | Strong | Strong | Only as good as the first pilots |
| **Capillary** | Slow: enterprise rollout | Some: has a rewards module | Some: aiRA builds campaigns, with a person approving each step | Some | Strong: 415+ brands, Al-Futtaim, just won Prince Retail |
| **Salesforce** | Slow: 6 to 18 months | None | Some: Agentforce creates promotions | Weak | Strong globally, thin in our markets |
| **Talon.One, Antavo** | Medium: 3 to 6 months, needs engineers | None | Some: Antavo's agents plan and report | None | Strong in European retail |
| **Almonds, BigCity, Channelplay** | Fast for their niche | Some: vouchers and bank payouts | None found | Strong: this is their home turf | Strong in India: Coca-Cola, Mahindra, Havells |
| **Loylty Rewardz** | Unknown | Strong: 25,000+ products, delivers to 15,000 PIN codes | None found | Some | Banks plus channel programs |
| **Xoxoday, Qwikcilver** | Fast for vouchers | Strong on vouchers, thin beyond them | None | Some (Alkem's app is built on Xoxoday) | Strong: 5,000+ companies |
| **Pointspay, Merit, Resal, Ascenda** | Fast | Strong in their niche | None | None | Strong with airlines, telcos and banks |

**Where we win in the room:**
- **Against the software players (Capillary, Salesforce, Talon.One):** they sell a points engine and leave the brand to find rewards. We bring the rewards and handle delivery, and our rewards-only offer goes live in weeks, not months.
- **Against the channel specialists (Almonds, BigCity):** they're good on the ground in India. But they're small (Almonds made ₹35 Cr), mostly India-only, and we found no sign they have agents that run the program. We offer deeper stock and one partner across India, the Gulf and Singapore.
- **Against the rewards suppliers (Xoxoday, Pointspay, Merit):** they supply rewards and stop there. We add the engagement layer on top.

**Where we lose today:**
- **No non-bank proof.** Every reference we have is a bank, and the first question in any room will be "who else like us uses you?" Until the first pilots are live, the answer is our bank scale plus a simple line: "same fulfilment, different points currency."
- **The agents are still on paper.** Capillary's aiRA and Antavo's agents are live now. We shouldn't promise agents in a pitch before month 4.
- **Channel specialists have the field relationships.** In dealer programs, they know the distributors personally. We start by working alongside them, not by trying to replace them.

**The one to watch is Capillary.** It's in every market we're targeting, it already has AI in the product, it has a rewards module, and it just won business in the Philippines. It's the most likely competitor in our biggest deals. For India specifically, Loylty Rewardz is the closest like-for-like rival, because it already has a big catalogue and channel programs.

I didn't research Vernost, Aspire or Collinson this session. They're worth a separate look before we pitch in their markets.

| Collinson, Ascenda-type players | Rewards, lounges, travel | The brand | Yes, but little AI |

**The pattern:** every one of them puts the AI in the marketer's hands, as a smarter dashboard. None gives each member their own assistant that knows their points and then redeems, books or cashes out once they say yes. That member-facing half is the Muse idea, and it's open ground.

**Where we're genuinely ahead**
1. **An assistant that acts for the member, not a dashboard for the brand.** No one on the list has this live.
2. **Software, agents and rewards in one box.** Salesforce, Antavo and Talon have no rewards. Collinson has rewards but little software. We have 17 years of catalogue, delivery and payouts at bank scale behind the software.

4. **Your recent products point the same way.** The agent-commerce app, where points are spent through AI agents, is ahead of what Eagle Eye is only preparing for.

**Where we're behind**
1. **Nothing is live.** Innovation counts when it ships. Antavo, GRAVTY and Eagle Eye are selling their AI today.
2. **The lead can close fast.** Salesforce already has Agentforce, and Capillary has aiRA and just bought scale. Either could add a member-facing assistant. My estimate is we have 6 to 12 months before this idea is crowded. That's my judgment, not a sourced number.
3. **No references outside banks yet.** Buyers will ask who's live.

**What moves us from "best idea" to "best product"**
1. **Ship first:** get three paying pilots live by month 3, with the assistant actually redeeming and paying out, not just chatting. Owner: CTO.
2. **Make our rewards visible to outside AI shoppers early.** Eagle Eye's argument that loyalty will be bought through AI agents is right, and our content makes it real. Owner: product lead.
3. **Turn the pilots into case studies within 30 days of launch.** Owner: CEO.

So on design we're ahead of the global field today. Whether we stay there comes down to having something live in three months.

### Turn 43 · Claude
*[Work: added a deterministic vs probabilistic section to the product doc]*

Yes, we're aligned. The rule is simple:

- **Deterministic:** anything that's a fact, moves points or money, or carries legal weight. It comes from our systems and rules, gives the same answer every time, and the AI only reads it out.
- **Probabilistic:** anything that's language, judgement or taste. The AI handles it, but only inside limits the deterministic side sets.

**Deterministic (the AI never decides these):**
- The points balance, points earned and expiry dates
- The member's tier, and what it takes to reach the next one
- Whether a member qualifies for an offer
- What any reward costs in points
- Whether a redemption, booking or payout went through, and for how much
- Tax, and order, delivery and refund status
- KYC and consent status
- Terms and legal wording, used word for word
- Fraud decisions and spend limits
- What the law allows in each market

**Probabilistic (the AI is allowed, with a limit on each):**
- **Working out what the member means, in any language.** If it isn't sure, it asks.
- **Wording and tone.** Every fact inside the reply still comes from the deterministic list.
- **Which offer to suggest.** It can only pick from offers the rules engine says the member qualifies for.
- **When to speak up.** Within the brand's message limits and the member's consent.
- **Explaining a rule in plain words.** The rule itself is quoted exactly.
- **Reading a bill photo.** Points are credited only if the numbers pass checks; otherwise a person looks at it.
- **Remembering what a member likes.** This never changes a balance or who qualifies for what.
- **Drafting playbooks for staff.** A person approves before anything goes live.

Where the two meet, the order never changes: the AI proposes, the system checks, and only then does anything happen. If a system can't be reached, the assistant says "I can't check that right now" and never guesses.

This is now a section in the product doc, under the intelligence layer, with both lists as tables.

The product name is still open: reply 1 for Tenzing, 2 for Remi, 3 for Norbu, or 4 and I'll bring five more.

### Turn 45 · Claude
*[Work: ran 2 name-check agents; 4 web searches on consumer AI assistant names and candidates]*

I looked at what consumer AI assistants are called today and ran two rounds of clash checks on 22 names. The short answer: every good real word is already taken in our markets, so the name has to be invented.

**What's out there, in three styles**
- **Person names:** Erica (Bank of America), Eno (Capital One), HDFC's EVA, Siri, Alexa. They feel warm, but each has a gender and a culture attached, and banks have already used most of them.
- **Character names:** Amazon's Rufus (named after an office dog), Walmart's Sparky, Expedia's Romie. They're playful and friendly, and they don't feel like a man or a woman. The newest shopping assistants are going this way.
- **Concept names:** Meta's Muse, Google's Gemini, Microsoft's Copilot. They sound premium and platform-like, but you don't chat with a concept.

**My recommendation: a character name, not a person and not a product word.**
- It works in India, the Gulf and South-East Asia without signalling gender, religion or region.
- People happily chat with it.
- Brands can keep it ("Lulu, with Kanzo") or rename it, with "powered by" underneath.
- Avoid the letters P and V, because Arabic speakers struggle with both.

**What the checks killed**
- Wink: a Singapore rewards app.
- Inaam: Nesto's loyalty app in the Gulf.
- Hala: a Saudi payments company.
- Lumi: a UAE AI company.
- Nomo: a Gulf digital bank.
- Zuno: an Indian insurer.
- Kiko: a cosmetics chain with a UAE loyalty program.
- Zumi, Moko and Tuki: existing AI apps.

**What survived**

1. **Kanzo.** It comes from *kanz*, Arabic for "treasure": the keeper of your treasure. It's friendly, easy to say in Hindi, Arabic and Malay, and I found no clash in AI, loyalty or payments. One small flag: in Japanese, *kanzō* means "liver". This is my pick.
2. **Bijou.** French for "little jewel". Nothing significant uses it, and it has no bad meaning in our languages. It feels more premium than playful.
3. **Karo.** Hindi for "do it", which is exactly what the assistant does. The catch: it could be confused with Indian cashback apps like CashKaro and EarnKaro.
4. **None of these.** I'll do a bolder round, for example a word or sound rather than a name, like the "psst" of someone giving you insider news.

Reply with a number. Before we lock anything, I'd run a formal trademark search in India, the UAE, Saudi Arabia and South-East Asia, and ask native speakers to check the name in Thai and Tagalog.

*[Work: 2 research subagents launched (AI vendor naming models; enterprise buying and branding)]*

I've done the research. Here's the answer and what it means for the name.

**What sells most easily to big companies: a product, white-labelled.**

1. **The customer never sees the vendor.** 13 of the 15 AI assistant vendors I checked sell a platform or product, and the big brand names the assistant itself:
   - Standard Chartered's "Stacy" runs on Kasisto.
   - RBC's "NOMI" runs on Personetics.
   - ABN AMRO's "Anna" runs on Microsoft.
   - SiriusXM's "Harmony" runs on Sierra.
   - Vodafone's "TOBi" kept its name when Vodafone moved it from IBM to OpenAI.
2. **Vendor-named characters get dropped.** IPsoft sold its own persona, "Amelia". SEB renamed it "Aida", and Nordnet cancelled it. Intercom's "Fin" is the only vendor name customers see by default, and even Intercom now lets each brand rename it.
3. **Brands won't pay to build loyalty to someone else's character.** McKinsey (April 2026) calls outside AI agents the biggest threat to banks' customer relationships. A persona with our name on it looks exactly like that threat. And the brand carries the legal risk for whatever the assistant says: Air Canada was held liable for its chatbot's wrong answer.
4. **"Platform" sounds like a build project, and buyers are wary of it.** Gartner expects over 40% of AI agent projects to be cancelled by 2027 for unclear value and cost. A finished product with a clear result is easier to approve.

**So the name we pick is a product name, and it's the name buyers see.** Brands sign for "Favour by R360". Their members meet "Lulu's assistant", or whatever name the brand chooses, with "powered by Favour" only if the brand wants it. The name has to impress a CMO or head of loyalty, not a shopper. Favour fits that well: it says what the product does, in plain words.

**What else, in the Favour spirit.** I checked 13 more names. Most are taken by AI or payments companies (Kindly, Gladly, Nod, Regards, Treat, Cheers, Savour, Due). Two are clean:
- **Courtesy.** It gives a nice line for brands: "courtesy of Lulu". It's less warm than Favour.
- **Gesture.** As in "a kind gesture". Warm, but softer, and it's also a tech term for gesture controls.

**Favour's one watch-out: Fave.** Fave is a big deals and cashback app in Malaysia and Singapore, and people will shorten Favour to "Fav". This matters less now that members won't usually see our name. We'd still need a trademark check in both countries.

**One more finding, on pricing.** Sierra charges only when a conversation is resolved. Ascenda charges banks only for results delivered, with no setup fee. Our doc leads with a monthly fee per active member. Leading with pay-for-results (per redemption, per member won back) would make the sale easier.

Reply with a number:

2. Go with Courtesy instead, on the same plan.
3. Keep looking for names.

### Turn 57 · Claude
*[Work: 3 web searches; fetched 3 pages (AwardWallet alternatives, Section 1033 status, Points about page)]*

**On the US question:** you can't just get the APIs from the top 50 programs. They don't offer them to outsiders, and many actively block anyone who tries to collect their data.

- **Airlines are the hardest.** American Airlines pulled out of AwardWallet, the biggest points tracker, entirely in 2023. Delta, United and Southwest only work there through a workaround where members forward their emails.
- **Logging in with members' passwords breaks the programs' terms.** Airlines have fought trackers over exactly this. It works until the program shuts you off.
- **Banks won't have to share either, for now.** The US rule that would force banks to share customer data with apps like ours (Section 1033) is blocked in court and being rewritten, so we can't plan around it.

**What does work in the US:**
1. **Signing deals with the programs.** Points (now part of Plusgrade) has commercial deals with big programs to buy, transfer and swap points. A US consumer app would be built through partners like them, or by signing programs one at a time. That's slow, but it lasts.
2. **Reading on the member's own device.** Some newer apps read a balance from a page the member is already logged into, through a browser extension, so no password is stored. It works, but it's fragile.
3. **Programs that choose to open up.** Some already let outside AI shopping agents read points (Talon.One's tools, for example). More will follow as AI shopping grows.

**My honest view: not now.** A consumer "all your points in one app" business is crowded and fragile, and the programs see it as a threat. That's the same fear that makes our B2B sale work: brands want their own assistant because they don't want a middleman between them and their members. If we're selling to those brands, a consumer app that sits on top of them makes us look like that middleman. It could make sense later, built on partner deals, or as an extension of your agent-commerce app idea.

**On the name,** here's the enterprise-style round. Of 10 names checked, most are taken in fintech or AI (Accrue, Keel, Lodestar, Steadfast, Orbis, Kinetiq, Loyra). Three are usable:
1. **Tenure.** Loyalty measured in time. It sounds like serious enterprise software and has no language issues. The nearest clash is Tenure Rewards, an employee-rewards company in North America, which is next to our market rather than in it.
2. **Merito.** "Merit" in Italian and Spanish, which helps in Europe. The only clashes are in unrelated software, plus a big Polish university brand called WSB Merito.
3. **Ardent.** Devoted, like an ardent fan. It sounds like Sierra or Decagon. There are AI tools with the name, but nothing in loyalty or finance, and it's a common word, so it's harder to own.

Reply with a number, or 4 for another round. All three need a formal trademark search before we commit.

You're right, the deck is thin on what the product actually does. It shows moments (Lisbon, cinema, missing points) but never spells out what "a personal agent for loyalty" means. It also barely says what the brand gets.

I'd add three slides after "Give every member their own assistant":

1. **"Think of it as Muse, for loyalty."** Meta launched Muse in September 2026 as a personal agent that knows you, keeps working in the background and gets things done once you say yes. This slide sets that frame in one line, then shows the five things the assistant does for each member:
   - **Knows:** points, tier, what's expiring and every offer that applies.
   - **Speaks up:** points about to expire, a target nearly hit, a price drop.
   - **Does it:** redeems, books, claims missing points.
   - **Remembers:** preferences and plans, which the member can see and wipe.
   - **Leaves them in charge:** every action is logged and can be reversed.

   Each one gets a small screen as proof, not just a line of text.
2. **"What your team gets."** Agents that do the daily work of a loyalty team:
   - **Designs and launches programmes:** sets up playbooks and offers.
   - **Runs offers:** manages them week to week and wins back quiet members.
   - **Answers questions:** "Which members went quiet this month?", answered in plain English.
   - **Moves your programme across:** brings members and points over from your current provider.

   All of this is approved and tracked in the control room. Today the deck says almost nothing about this.
3. **"One conversation, start to finish."** A single member request followed end to end: what they ask, what the assistant checks, what it proposes, their yes, and the booking done. This is where the depth lands.

The Gratifi home screen is ready for you to review, as two boards on the canvas: the first screen a member sees, and the full scroll.

**How it's laid out, and why:**
- **It opens as a sheet over the bank's app.** The host app stays visible behind it, so the member never feels they've left. It works the same way as Apple Pay and Stripe's checkout sheet.
- **The top card shows three things:** the points balance, a progress ring towards the next tier, and a "Use points" button. The line above it says how far they are from Platinum, which gives them a reason to act.
- **The assistant sits in the search bar and is labelled "Ask about your points".** There's no invented name and no button that's just a sparkle icon. Amazon's Rufus research and NN/g both found that a plain label gets far more use.
- **The assistant speaks up with an offer, not a chat bubble.** The yellow note is the assistant flagging expiring points: "2,400 points run out this weekend. They cover two cinema tickets." The member either redeems in one tap or says "Not now".
- **Ways to spend come next:** a row of category stamps, with a "2×" sticker on the boosted one. The last stamp is cut off at the edge so it's obvious the row scrolls.
- **The full scroll adds four sections:**
  - **Coming up:** the booked Lisbon flight.
  - **Book again:** repeat orders.
  - **Picked for you:** a Lisbon hotel to go with that flight, and a jacket.
  - **Recent activity:** includes "Missing points claimed · by your assistant", so members see what the assistant did for them.

I've fixed the blank strip at the bottom of the installed app, but you need to reinstall it once for the fix to show. I haven't tested it on a real iPhone.

**What caused it:** iOS makes the installed app's screen shorter than the phone when the app asks for a see-through status bar, which mine did. The bottom strip ends up outside the app, so nothing we draw can reach it. It's a known iOS 26 issue. I've switched to a solid status bar, which gives the app the full height. I also removed a height setting I'd added last round that was making it worse.

**What you need to do, once:** iPhone keeps the app's screen settings from the day you installed it, so reopening won't pick up the fix.
1. Press and hold the Gratifi icon on your home screen and delete it.
2. Open https://project-95d8n.vercel.app in Safari.
3. Tap Share, then Add to Home Screen.
4. Open it from the new icon.

The bank home, the Gratifi home and every other screen should now reach the bottom edge, with the AI bar just above the home line.

**What looks different:** the status bar at the top is now a solid strip in the system colour, not your app's colour showing through. That's the trade-off for filling the screen. If the bottom is still off after reinstalling, send me a screenshot.

Sources: [MindSpace fix](https://github.com/bosskantaponguttarawichien-boop/MindSpace/pull/21), [airtag-sentry fix](https://github.com/mariusgassen/airtag-sentry/pull/23), [busssss iOS 26 notes](https://github.com/tpdbf5509/busssss/pull/23)

The architecture doc is ready, with the full sitemap on its own tab.

- **Intelligence layer.** It's drawn step by step. The bank's systems and rules decide every number and every action. The model only works out what the customer is asking and writes the words, and a final check blocks any number that didn't come from the bank's systems.

I've written up how we do it in a new doc, "Commerce in chat: covering every use case".

We don't hand-design 1,000 flight screens. We build one machine that turns out use cases, and every category and new supplier runs through it:

- **Use cases are generated, not written.** Each one is a mix of four lists: what the customer wants done, where they are in the trip, what limits them (budget, dates, points), and what can go wrong. Flights give well over a thousand combinations. We score them by how often they happen, what they're worth and the cost of a mistake, and build the top ones first. Anything rare hands to a person with the whole conversation attached.
- **One kit of about 60 building blocks for the screens.** The agent picks a block and fills it in; it never draws a screen. That keeps every category looking like one product and lets a bank put its own brand on the lot. The doc shows three flight blocks as they'd appear on screen, including a price change and a cancellation.
- **One agent per category, all built the same way.** Each gets:
  - the suppliers' tools
  - a record of what each supplier can really do
  - one set of steps per use case
  - the policies it can quote
  - rules it must never break
- **Tested before any customer sees it.** Simulated customers run thousands of conversations every night, checked automatically, with a person reviewing a sample each week. We train small models only later, once there's real traffic, so the main model stays swappable.
- **New MCPs go live in days.** Each new supplier is checked automatically for what it can do and inherits its category's use cases, blocks and tests. Only its own quirks are new work.

The doc also lists what every R360 project already brings: Open Rewards, TripSure, PHANTOM, Airport VIP, iRewards, Piingle and ZERO. It covers all 18 categories, gives the flights lists in full with the first 20 use cases to build, and lists the risks, each with its fix and owner.

It ends with four questions. The one that matters most is whether we can use PHANTOM's conversation and ticket history, with HDFC's written permission. It's the best training material we have for flights.

Next I can build the full flight use-case list as a working spreadsheet, and the first flight screens for you to approve.

### Turn 165 · Claude
*[Work: read the architecture doc; added the "One product, many banks" section with a drawing of the shared core and walled bank instances; checked the drawing]*

Every bank gets the same product and nobody gets a fork. I've added this as a new section, "One product, many banks", in the architecture doc, with a drawing.

**Same code for every bank.** The engine, the guardrails, the security controls, the UI kit, the system that produces use cases and the model gateway are identical everywhere. That's why a fix or a new feature reaches every bank at once.

**Tailoring goes through four dials**, from things the bank changes itself to things our engineers build:
1. **Settings.** The bank's own team changes these in an admin console, and a second person approves each change: brand, the assistant's name and tone, which areas are switched on, rules and limits, and which model tier they pay for.
2. **Content.** Their cards, terms, benefits, offers and help articles, whether R360 content is on, and their own test questions.
3. **Connections to their systems.** Links to their core banking, card platform, rewards system and sign-in. We build one per system vendor, so the second bank on the same card platform costs far less than the first.
4. **Extensions.** Journeys only that bank wants. We build them on the same UI kit but keep them outside the shared code. When two or more banks want the same one, it moves into the shared product.

Anything a bank asks for that doesn't fit these four becomes a feature request for the shared product, never a separate version for them. We sort every request into a dial when we sign the contract, and the contract says so.

**Bank one and bank two never meet.** Each bank gets its own copy, usually in its own cloud, with its own keys, database, customer memory and logs. No customer data, conversation or learning passes between banks, or back to R360, unless the bank agrees in writing. What they share is what we build centrally: the code, the UI kit, the standard steps for each use case, and the test suites, which are built from made-up data.

**Updates.** Everyone is on one release schedule. Each bank takes a new release only after it passes that bank's own test questions, at a time the bank picks. No bank may fall more than two releases behind, so we never end up maintaining old versions.

The section also covers the five steps to bring a new bank on, and the risks, each with its fix and owner. The biggest risk is a large bank insisting on changes to the shared code. The answer is to build it as an extension now and add it to the product later if others want it.

Next, I can mock up the admin console where a bank sets these dials. It's what their team would see in a demo.

### Turn 167 · Claude
*[Work: read the architecture doc; added the "Large banks and small banks" section]*

Agreed, and it doesn't need a separate product. I've added a "Large banks and small banks" section to the architecture doc.

**Three things separate a big bank from a small one:**

1. **How it's run.** Enterprise banks get Gratifi in their own cloud or on their own hardware, the premium model, a named team and their own update timing. Small banks get a managed copy from ready-made templates and go live in days to weeks.
2. **Exclusivity, limited by time and market.** A big bank buys first access, never its own version of the product:
   - new features and categories go to them first for an agreed period
   - anything we build with them stays theirs in their market for that period, then joins the product
   - R360 can sign content, such as a hotel or airline partner, exclusively for one bank in a market

   Every exclusivity has an end date and a market, so Barclays' deal in the UK never blocks a bank in Singapore.
3. **Their data, which is where the real difference comes from.** The code is the same everywhere; what each copy learns is not. Each bank's Gratifi learns only from that bank's data, inside its own walls:
   - transactions
   - account events
   - the rewards ledger
   - app behaviour
   - customer records and product holdings
   - campaign history
   - call-centre transcripts

   Over time the same product behaves differently at every bank, and the bank owns what was learned. If it leaves, that learning goes with it or is deleted. A CIO will want that promise in writing.

Small banks with little data start from R360's ready-made defaults, so they get something useful on day one. The section also covers the risks, each with its fix and owner.

One decision for you. When a big bank gets first access to a new feature, co-built journey or content deal, how long should that exclusivity last before every other bank gets it?
1. Six months: easier to sell to other banks sooner
2. Twelve months: a stronger reason for a big bank to sign
3. Set per deal: flexible, but harder to keep track of

### Turn 208 · Amit
*Sent: Thu 1 Oct 2026, 13:55 IST*
Explain me the idea summary

### Turn 209 · Claude
*[Work: read 2 memory files]*

**Gratifi, in one line:** an AI assistant that a bank builds into its card app. Customers just say what they want, and their points and card get it done.

**The problem.** Bank reward programmes are hard to use. Customers don't know what their points are worth, the catalogues are clunky, and most people end up redeeming for vouchers or not at all. Card servicing (freeze my card, why was this declined, pay my bill) sits in a separate maze of menus.

**The idea.** Replace the menus with one assistant that does everything:
- **Spend points and card on anything:** flights, hotels, rides, dining, 15-minute groceries, shopping, gift cards, subscriptions, events, experiences.
- **Manage points:** transfer to airline miles, grow them through a licensed partner, donate them.
- **Run the card:** pay the bill, freeze it, set limits, dispute charges, report fraud.
- **Look after people:** spot scams, pressure from someone else, money worries and crisis moments, and route to real help.

The customer types "two seats to Dubai next Friday, use my points". The assistant shows options as familiar app-style cards, and the customer taps once to confirm. Nothing moves without that confirmation, in the bank's own approval step.

**How the app is laid out.** Home and Explore are the shop window: banners, offers and featured products that pull people in. The real work happens in the conversation.

**Who it's for.** It's a business-to-business product. Banks put it inside their app under their own brand, as "Gratifi by R360". It runs on each bank's own rewards, rules and data, and R360's global rewards content can come on top. Each bank's version is kept fully separate.

**Why it matters for R360.** It moves R360 from running reward catalogues to owning the customer's everyday experience with their card. It also shifts redemptions toward travel, experiences and products rather than low-margin vouchers. As more brands open up their systems to AI assistants, more content can plug straight in.

**Where it stands.** There's a working prototype in 7 markets: UK, EU, India, UAE, UAE in Arabic, Singapore and Malaysia. It covers 19 categories and runs on a mock bank, with the gaps listed in the handoff doc.

| Area | Category | What a customer can do |
|---|---|---|
| Travel | Flights | Search by date, passengers and time of day. Pick fares, seats and bags, then pay. Change, cancel or rebook if the airline cancels. Boarding passes appear when check-in opens. |
| Travel | Stays | Find hotels by city, dates, rooms and room type. Book with free cancellation, cancel or change. |
| Travel | Airport | Book lounges (free visits used first), fast track, meet and greet. |
| Travel | Rides and rail | Book a ride now or later from here, home or office. Car hire by days. Trains where the market has them. |
| Travel | Experiences | Tours and tickets in any city, by day and time. |
| Travel | Travel essentials | Visa check, travel cover, eSIM data. |
| Everyday | Dining | Free table booking. Groups of 13 or more go to concierge. |
| Everyday | Groceries | 15-minute delivery. Add, remove or change items by text or buttons. Refunds for missing items. |
| Everyday | Shopping | Products with sizes, returns and order tracking. |
| Everyday | Gift cards | Fixed amounts, sent to a recipient's email. |
| Everyday | Subscriptions | Included and paid plans. Start, pause, resume, cancel. |
| Everyday | Events | Gigs, sport, theatre. 2-for-1 cinema. |
| Points | Points and miles | Balance, history, transfer to airline programmes. |
| Points | Grow points | Gold, funds and savings through a licensed partner, with a risk note. |
| Points | Give | Donate points to charities. |
| Points | Challenges | Join and track earning challenges. |
| Card | My card | Balance, statement, pay bill, Direct Debit, freeze, controls, limits, gambling block. Disputes, lost or stolen card, fraud, card abroad, instalments, balance transfers. |
| Card | Card benefits | What the card covers, money back, no foreign fee. |
| Help | Concierge | Anything else, such as sold-out tables or special gifts, handed to a person. |
| Care | Safety (always on) | Spots crisis, scams, coercion, financial abuse and emergencies in English and Arabic, and gives the right helpline or emergency number. |
| Everywhere | Markets | UK, EU (Ireland), India, UAE, UAE in Arabic (right to left), Singapore, Malaysia, each with local money, prices and payment approval. |
| Everywhere | Guardrails | Nothing moves money, points or card settings without a confirm step. Every payment is checked against available credit. |

It depends on which link you open.

1. **The Vercel link (project-95d8n.vercel.app): no AI model at all right now.** It runs on Gratifi's built-in engine. That's our own rules and flows: it reads what you type, matches it to the right flow, and shows the cards. It's fast and predictable, but it isn't an LLM. The Live AI mode can't connect there, because there's no server holding an API key.

2. **The Claude prototype link: Claude, by Anthropic.** Live AI asks Claude for each answer:
   - Short, simple messages go to Claude's fast tier, and longer ones with several requests go to the standard tier. Claude picks the exact model version behind each tier, and the Claude account of whoever is using the page pays for it.
   - Every message first goes through a separate safety check, also run by Claude, before any answer is shown.
   - Claude never moves money itself. It can only use the app's own tools (search, show options, open a checkout), and every payment still needs the person to confirm.

**For production:** to get Live AI on the Vercel link or in a bank's app, we add a small server that calls the Claude API directly, using a fast model for routine requests and a stronger one for complex ones. Only that one piece changes. The tools, the safety layers and the module filter all stay as they are.

Every test passed in all 7 markets: hundreds of phrases per market and 140 end-to-end journeys, with no Arabic missing.

**Is the AI layer best in industry?** For a prototype, the design patterns are at the level of the best published approaches. It is not yet production-grade. Here is the comparison:

| Area | What Gratifi does | Measured against | Where it stands |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| How the agent works | Claude can only call the app's own tools, and every payment waits for the customer's confirmation | Anthropic's agent guidance, Apple's confirm step for money | At par |
| Safety | A fixed check runs first, then a second Claude check runs alongside the answer and can replace it | Parallel safety checks in OpenAI's agent toolkit | At par or ahead |
| Truthfulness | Any sentence saying "booked" or "paid" is removed unless it really happened | Few products do this | Ahead |
| What appears on screen | Claude picks only from approved cards; it never draws its own UI | Vercel's AI toolkit, Google's agent-to-UI approach | At par |
| Cost and speed | Fast model for simple messages, stronger one for complex; rules cached | Standard best practice | At par |
| Fallback | The built-in engine answers if the AI is off or fails | Bank assistants like Erica are mostly rule-based | Ahead of most |
| Bank modules | Services switch on only when the bank offers the APIs, in screens and answers alike | Per-client feature switches | At par |

What stands between this and production, with a fix and an owner for each:

1. **Tools run in the browser on demo data.** Fix: move them onto a server against the bank's APIs, with an audit log and protection against double payments. Owner: R360 engineering.
2. **Anyone with the link can use the Claude connection.** Fix: you set the spend limit now, and later the server only accepts signed-in bank customers. Owner: you today, engineering later.
3. **The AI's instructions are put together in the browser.** Fix: build them on the server so they can't be tampered with. Owner: engineering.
4. **The AI isn't tested automatically.** Fix: a graded set of conversations, scored on every release. Owner: engineering with product.
5. **Nothing traces or reports the AI's activity.** Fix: tracing of model calls with personal data stripped, plus cost dashboards. Owner: engineering.
6. **Partner content could carry hidden instructions** once real partner feeds come in. Fix: clean partner text before Claude sees it. Owner: engineering.
7. **No regulatory sign-off.** Fix: compliance review and model-risk paperwork for each bank. Owner: R360 compliance.

The prototype page is republished with the same changes. The handoff doc and README now explain the Claude connection.

*[Work: fetched the Claude rate-limits docs page]*

**Adding the Claude key takes about 5 minutes, on a laptop:**

1. **Get a key.** Go to platform.claude.com and sign in with your Anthropic account. Add a payment card under Settings → Billing. Then open Settings → API keys, click **Create key**, name it "Gratifi" and copy it. It's shown only once.
2. **Set a spend cap.** Still under Settings → Billing, find **Spend limits** and set a monthly cap, for example $50. Keep it low while we test.
3. **Put the key in Vercel.** Open vercel.com → project **project-95d8n** → Settings → Environment Variables. Add one with:
   - Name: `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`
   - Value: the key you copied
   - Environments: tick all three
   Click Save.
4. **Redeploy.** Go to Deployments, open the "…" menu on the top deployment and click **Redeploy**.

Tell me when it's done. I'll check the link, confirm Claude is answering, and run a test chat.

Don't paste the key here in the chat. It only goes into Vercel.

Here's how to add the key. Use a laptop; it takes about 5 minutes.

**Part A: Get the key from Anthropic**

1. Go to **platform.claude.com** and sign in. Use your email if you don't have an account yet.
2. Click **Settings**, then **Billing**, and add a payment card.
3. On the same page, find **Spend limits** and set a low monthly limit, for example $50.
4. Click **Settings**, then **API keys**, then **Create key**.
5. Name it **Gratifi** and click create.
6. Copy the key. It's a long line starting with `sk-ant-`. It's shown only once, so keep the page open until Part B is done.

**Part B: Put the key into Vercel**

1. Go to **vercel.com** and sign in.
2. Open the project **project-95d8n**.
3. Click **Settings** at the top, then **Environment Variables** on the left.
4. In **Key**, type exactly `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`
5. In **Value**, paste the key you copied.
6. Leave all three boxes ticked (Production, Preview, Development).
7. Click **Save**.

**Part C: Restart the app so it picks up the key**

1. Click **Deployments** at the top.
2. On the first row, click the **three dots (…)** on the right.
3. Click **Redeploy**, then **Redeploy** again to confirm.
4. Wait about a minute until it says **Ready**.

Then reply "done" here and I'll check that Claude is answering and run a test chat.

Don't send me the key in this chat. It only goes into Vercel.

So if I want to set the limit on my card on domestic spend versus international spend and so on and so forth, generally what card, you know, a good credit card company offers in terms of controls on a card is everything covered.

### Turn 363 · Claude
*[Work: 2 shell commands]*

Yes. Domestic versus international is covered, with separate daily limits at home and abroad. So is almost everything a good card company offers today.

**What a customer can already do**
- **Freeze:** freeze and unfreeze the card instantly.
- **Where it works:**
  - Turn domestic and international payments on or off.
  - Add travel notices.
- **How it's used:** turn each of these on or off: online, in store, contactless, cash withdrawals and phone wallets.
- **Limits:**
  - a monthly limit,
  - a limit on each payment,
  - limits by category,
  - daily limits for each of those ways to pay, set separately at home and abroad.
  - Lowering a limit is instant. Raising one asks the customer to confirm.
- **Spending blocks:**
  - gambling,
  - crypto,
  - money transfers and e-wallet top-ups,
  - premium-rate phone lines,
  - adult sites.
- **Alerts:** an alert on every payment or over a set amount, plus declined-payment alerts, a statement-ready alert and a bill reminder (1, 3 or 7 days before).
- **PIN:** show the PIN in the app; the app points people to the bank for a PIN change.
- **When something goes wrong:** report fraud, get a replacement card, and the app explains any declined payment with a one-tap fix.

**What leading card apps have that we don't yet**
1. **Virtual card numbers:** a separate or single-use number for shopping online.
2. **Controls on add-on cards:** limits and blocks on a family member's card.
3. **Blocking one merchant or stopping a repeat payment:** for example, stopping a gym that keeps charging.
4. **Choosing which countries the card works in**, instead of all abroad on or off.
5. **Showing the card number and CVV** in the app.

Of these, 2 and 3 matter most to banks. Want me to build them? Reply with the numbers you want, or "all".

### Turn 364 · Amit
*Sent: Sat 3 Oct 2026, 19:59 IST*
Super. Another thing you got to remember is you got to follow actual proper protocol that a bank would follow. ### Turn 365 · Claude
*[Work: 8 shell commands]*

Yes. Bank-grade authentication is already built in, and it follows the rule banks use: anything that adds risk needs the customer to prove it's them, and anything that protects them works instantly.

**Needs the customer to confirm it's them (Face ID, a one-time code or the bank's own app, depending on the market)**
- Unfreezing the card
- Switching anything back on: international, domestic, online, in store, contactless, cash
- Raising any limit
- Lifting a spending block
- Showing the PIN or the card details
- Changing the due date, setting up a payment plan, every payment and booking

The one-time code locks for 15 minutes after three wrong tries. A cancelled card can't be unfrozen at all.

**Works instantly, no code (this is what banks do, so a customer can protect themselves fast)**
- Freezing the card
- Switching anything off
- Lowering a limit
- Adding a spending block

**Handed to the bank:** address and contact details. The bank holds those, so the app sends the customer to the bank's app or to a person.

**One gap I found while checking:** turning off payment alerts doesn't ask for confirmation. Banks lock this down, because it's often the first thing a fraudster does after taking over an account.

**A correction to my last message:** the card number and CVV can already be shown in the app, behind confirmation. So four controls are missing, not five.

Want me to build these in one go?
1. Add confirmation for turning off alerts
2. Build controls on add-on cards
3. Build blocking one merchant and stopping a repeat payment
4. All three
